========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. It's nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess what brings me in today is... it's work stuff, mostly. I work at a meat processing plant, and honestly, the job itself is fine, you know? But there's all this paperwork and protocol, and I just... I keep messing it up. Like, not the actual processing part, but the follow-through. I'll start the sanitation steps—which are super strict, obviously, because of health codes and everything—and I'll get through the first few parts, getting all the equipment sprayed down and whatever, and then somehow, I just... I leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! I'll start filling out the batch logs for the shift, writing down the weights and the times, and then I'll get distracted or I'll just sort of stop before it's actually finished. It's the same with the end-of-line paperwork. I'll have the clipboard right there, I'll start checking off the boxes, and then I'll just walk away or forget to sign off on the bottom section. And it's so frustrating because I *know* my job depends on finishing them correctly. Like, my supervisor has talked to me about it, and I sit there thinking, "I know I have to do this, I know how important it is," but then the next day, I do the exact same thing. It's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time, and I don't know why I can't just push through and finish the whole task.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow, yeah. Actually, yes, absolutely. It’s funny you ask that because it’s not just a new thing with the plant, even though the stakes feel a lot higher now with health inspectors and everything. But no, it goes way, way back. Like, my mom actually still has this whole box of my old school stuff in her basement—she keeps everything, you know how moms are—and she was just looking through it recently. She pulled out these old report cards from elementary school, I think specifically around fourth grade? And she was reading them to me, and it was honestly a little embarrassing, but also very validating, I guess? 

Every single teacher wrote almost the exact same thing. They’d say I was bright, that I caught on quickly, which was nice to hear, but then there was always a "but." It was always, "She needs constant reminders to finish her classwork," or "She struggles to turn in homework." And I remember that so clearly. I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. But when it came time to actually do the worksheet or write the paragraph, I’d just... drift. I’d miss whole chunks of instructions. The teacher would say, "Do steps one through five," and I’d somehow only hear step one, or I’d do step one and two and then just completely forget there were three more steps. Unless the teacher was physically hovering near my desk, tapping on my paper or specifically checking in on me, the work just didn’t get finished. It’s like, if they walked away, my brain just went, "Okay, we're done here," even if the paper was mostly blank. So, yeah, it’s definitely not just the batch logs at work... it’s been happening basically my whole life.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I wasn't one of those kids who was always jumping out of their seat or running around the classroom, you know? Like, I remember there was this one boy in my second-grade class—Tommy, I think his name was—and he literally could not stay in his chair. He was always up at the pencil sharpener, or looking out the window, or just wandering around the back of the room by the cubbies, and the teacher was constantly having to tell him to sit back down. But that wasn't me at all. 

I was actually pretty quiet and still, physically speaking. I would sit right there at my desk, exactly where I was supposed to be. The problem wasn't my body getting up and leaving the chair, it was my brain getting up and leaving the room, if that makes sense. Like, I’d be sitting there, hands folded, looking totally compliant, but inside my head, I was a million miles away, thinking about a book I read, or what I was going to do after school, or just staring at the grain on the wooden desk. So, yeah, I never really got in trouble for getting out of my seat or being disruptive like that. I could sit there for the whole hour, no problem... I just wouldn't necessarily be doing what I was supposed to be doing while I was sitting there. But physically staying in the chair? Yeah, I was fine with that.

I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. It's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time, and I don't know why I can't just push through and finish the whole task.\n\nDoctor: Before you started this job, did you have the same kind of trouble finishing tasks when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. She pulled out these old report cards from elementary school, I think specifically around fourth grade? I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. But when it came time to actually do the worksheet or write the paragraph, I’d just... drift. I’d miss whole chunks of instructions. The teacher would say, \"Do steps one through five,\" and I’d somehow only hear step one, or I’d do step one and two and then just completely forget there were three more steps. I mean, I wasn't one of those kids who was always jumping out of their seat or running around the classroom, you know? He was always up at the pencil sharpener, or looking out the window, or just wandering around the back of the room by the cubbies, and the teacher was constantly having to tell him to sit back down. But that wasn't me at all. \n\nI was actually pretty quiet and still, physically speaking. I would sit right there at my desk, exactly where I was supposed to be. The problem wasn't my body getting up and leaving the chair, it was my brain getting up and leaving the room, if that makes sense. Outside of work? Um... you know, I don't really think so? Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being an issue like it is at the plant. It's funny because you'd think it would be exactly the same, right? Like, my brain is my brain no matter where I am. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Nervous? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit there panicking or anything like that. It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating over the clipboard. Which, by the way, the clipboard is always freezing cold anyway because my station is right near the blast freezers. I work with the poultry batches, so obviously everything has to be kept at exactly the right temperature to prevent bacteria, which is exactly why there are so many logs to fill out in the first place. Every thirty minutes, someone has to sign off on the ambient room temp and the internal meat temp, and it's just this endless stack of paper. 

But when I'm actually holding that clipboard, I'm not... I'm not worried about it. I'm not sitting there thinking, "Oh no, what if I mess this up?" or "What if the supervisor yells at me?" I know exactly *how* to do the paperwork. It's literally just writing down numbers and checking boxes. It's more like... it's just so incredibly tedious that my brain just slides right off the page. 

I'll be looking at the little grids where I'm supposed to write the time and the lot number, and instead of feeling anxious, I just feel... I don't know, completely disconnected? Like I'll start looking at the condensation dripping down the metal walls, or I'll start calculating how many chickens probably go through the line in a single twelve-hour shift, or I'll go back to thinking about the color-coded plates in my kitchen cabinets. There's no fear or worry there at all. Just a complete lack of traction. 

I know some of the other workers get really stressed about the compliance paperwork because the USDA inspectors are super strict, and if you miss a line or smudge a number, it can be this huge ordeal for the whole shift. But honestly, I don't get that nervous energy about it. I just literally cannot keep my eyes and my mind on the paper long enough to finish the column without zoning out completely. So, yeah, no anxiety or worrying, just... just a lot of staring off into space while holding a really cold pen.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
In college? Um, well, college was... I mean, I got my bachelor's degree, obviously, so I got through it. But it wasn't really the same kind of work, you know? Like, writing a paper about agricultural economics is a lot different than standing in a freezing room with a clipboard trying to remember if I checked the ambient temp at 10:15 or 10:30. In college, I could sort of hyper-focus on things if I was interested in them, or I'd just pull an all-nighter right before the deadline. It was stressful, but I got it done.

But what's happening now at the plant is just... it's why I'm here, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep starting the sanitation steps, the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and somehow I just leave out the last parts. And it's not like I don't know how important it is! I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. The USDA does not mess around, and if they pull our logs and see blank spaces or missing signatures, the whole line can get shut down, which costs the company thousands of dollars a minute. I know all of this. I've sat through the training videos, I've read the manuals, I know exactly what's at stake. 

But I'll be standing there, doing the sanitation check—which involves making sure every single surface on the processing line is clear of debris before the next batch of poultry comes through—and I'll check the main conveyor, the side rails, the catch pans, and then... I just stop. I'll walk away to grab a new pair of gloves, or I'll look at the clock, and I just completely forget to check the underneath of the belt, which is literally the last step. Or with the batch logs, I'll fill out the time, the lot number, the initial temp, the secondary temp, and then I just... don't sign my name at the bottom. I just leave it blank and put the clipboard back. 

It's so incredibly frustrating because I am not a stupid person. I have a degree! I know how to fill out a form. But it's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time. And my supervisor, Dave, he's been getting really annoyed with me. He pulled me aside last week and was like, "You're doing 90% of the work and failing the assignment," which, ironically, sounds like something a college professor would say, but Dave is this big guy with a huge beard who used to work on oil rigs, so it's a lot more intimidating coming from him. But he's right! I keep starting these crucial paperwork tasks and just... not finishing them, even though I know I could get fired for it. It's just bizarre, and I don't know why I keep doing it.

I'll wash all the dishes, dry them, put them away in the cabinets. I actually organized my plates and bowls by color a few months ago after I finished my bachelor's degree and finally had some free time. I didn't get halfway through the dishes and wander off to reorganize my closet or something. Or like, paying bills. I sit down at my laptop, open up the tabs for the electric company and my internet provider, and I just pay them. I don't get distracted by, you know, an article about how to propagate succulents, which is something I've been trying to do lately, but that's beside the point. \n\nThe point is, when I'm at home, or running errands, or just doing my own thing, I don't have that feeling of... of my brain just slipping off the track, I guess? I can make a grocery list, go to the store, get everything on the list, and come home. It's really just at the plant, with the batch logs and the temperature checks and the sanitation records. It's so frustrating because it's the one place where I really, really need to be on top of things, and it's the one place where I just can't seem to finish what I start. But yeah, outside of work? No, I'd say I'm pretty okay. I don't really struggle with it in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing the paperwork at work, do you find yourself feeling very nervous or worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit there panicking or anything like that. It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating over the clipboard. Which, by the way, the clipboard is always freezing cold anyway because my station is right near the blast freezers. I work with the poultry batches, so obviously everything has to be kept at exactly the right temperature to prevent bacteria, which is exactly why there are so many logs to fill out in the first place. Every thirty minutes, someone has to sign off on the ambient room temp and the internal meat temp, and it's just this endless stack of paper. \n\nBut when I'm actually holding that clipboard, I'm not... I'm not worried about it. I'm not sitting there thinking, \"Oh no, what if I mess this up?\" or \"What if the supervisor yells at me?\" I know exactly *how* to do the paperwork. It's literally just writing down numbers and checking boxes. It's more like... it's just so incredibly tedious that my brain just slides right off the page. \n\nI'll be looking at the little grids where I'm supposed to write the time and the lot number, and instead of feeling anxious, I just feel... I don't know, completely disconnected? Like I'll start looking at the condensation dripping down the metal walls, or I'll start calculating how many chickens probably go through the line in a single twelve-hour shift, or I'll go back to thinking about the color-coded plates in my kitchen cabinets. There's no fear or worry there at all. Just a complete lack of traction. \n\nI know some of the other workers get really stressed about the compliance paperwork because the USDA inspectors are super strict, and if you miss a line or smudge a number, it can be this huge ordeal for the whole shift. But honestly, I don't get that nervous energy about it. I just literally cannot keep my eyes and my mind on the paper long enough to finish the column without zoning out completely. So, yeah, no anxiety or worrying, just... just a lot of staring off into space while holding a really cold pen.\n\nDoctor: In college, did you often have the same trouble finishing assignments or turning in work on time?\n\nPatient: In college? Um, well, college was... I mean, I got my bachelor's degree, obviously, so I got through it. But it wasn't really the same kind of work, you know? Like, writing a paper about agricultural economics is a lot different than standing in a freezing room with a clipboard trying to remember if I checked the ambient temp at 10:15 or 10:30. In college, I could sort of hyper-focus on things if I was interested in them, or I'd just pull an all-nighter right before the deadline. It was stressful, but I got it done.\n\nBut what's happening now at the plant is just... it's why I'm here, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep starting the sanitation steps, the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and somehow I just leave out the last parts. And it's not like I don't know how important it is! I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. The USDA does not mess around, and if they pull our logs and see blank spaces or missing signatures, the whole line can get shut down, which costs the company thousands of dollars a minute. I know all of this. I've sat through the training videos, I've read the manuals, I know exactly what's at stake. \n\nBut I'll be standing there, doing the sanitation check—which involves making sure every single surface on the processing line is clear of debris before the next batch of poultry comes through—and I'll check the main conveyor, the side rails, the catch pans, and then... I just stop. I'll walk away to grab a new pair of gloves, or I'll look at the clock, and I just completely forget to check the underneath of the belt, which is literally the last step. Or with the batch logs, I'll fill out the time, the lot number, the initial temp, the secondary temp, and then I just... don't sign my name at the bottom. I just leave it blank and put the clipboard back. \n\nIt's so incredibly frustrating because I am not a stupid person. I have a degree! I know how to fill out a form. But it's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time. And my supervisor, Dave, he's been getting really annoyed with me. He pulled me aside last week and was like, \"You're doing 90% of the work and failing the assignment,\" which, ironically, sounds like something a college professor would say, but Dave is this big guy with a huge beard who used to work on oil rigs, so it's a lot more intimidating coming from him. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
As a child? Um... outside of school... you know, I really don't think so. I mean, if I really think back to when I was little, like elementary and middle school age, I don't remember having any issues like what I'm dealing with now at the plant. 

At home, for example, my mom was super strict about chores. We had this whole chore wheel on the fridge—she made it out of construction paper and laminated it, with a little metal brad in the middle so it could spin—and if it was my week to vacuum the living room, I just... vacuumed the whole living room. I didn't leave a corner undone or forget to put the vacuum away in the hall closet. I'd actually make sure the carpet had those nice even lines in it, you know? Or like, my extracurriculars. I took gymnastics for a few years, which I was honestly terrible at because I have absolutely zero upper body strength and I was terrified of the balance beam, but I never had trouble paying attention to the coach or finishing the routines. I didn't just wander off the mat halfway through a floor exercise. 

It's so weird to think about, because you'd assume if my brain worked this way, it would have shown up everywhere back then, right? But it really didn't. Whether I was at home doing my chores, or at my grandparents' house in the summer helping my grandpa in his garden, or at gymnastics, I was completely fine. I used to play board games with my younger brother all the time—Monopoly, mostly, which is the worst game because it takes literally hours and hours and always ends in an argument—but I'd sit there the entire time, totally focused, until someone won or one of us flipped the board in frustration. I never just lost the thread of what we were doing. 

So, no, I really don't remember any problems with my attention causing trouble for me at home or anywhere else like that when I was growing up. My parents never had to sit me down and tell me I was leaving things half-finished. It really just feels like this bizarre wall I'm hitting specifically with these batch logs and sanitation checks at work.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. It's nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess what brings me in today is... it's work stuff, mostly. I work at a meat processing plant, and honestly, the job itself is fine, you know? But there's all this paperwork and protocol, and I just... I keep messing it up. Like, not the actual processing part, but the follow-through. I'll start the sanitation steps—which are super strict, obviously, because of health codes and everything—and I'll get through the first few parts, getting all the equipment sprayed down and whatever, and then somehow, I just... I leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! I'll start filling out the batch logs for the shift, writing down the weights and the times, and then I'll get distracted or I'll just sort of stop before it's actually finished. It's the same with the end-of-line paperwork. I'll have the clipboard right there, I'll start checking off the boxes, and then I'll just walk away or forget to sign off on the bottom section. And it's so frustrating because I *know* my job depends on finishing them correctly. Like, my supervisor has talked to me about it, and I sit there thinking, \"I know I have to do this, I know how important it is,\" but then the next day, I do the exact same thing. It's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time, and I don't know why I can't just push through and finish the whole task.\n\nDoctor: Before you started this job, did you have the same kind of trouble finishing tasks when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. Actually, yes, absolutely. It’s funny you ask that because it’s not just a new thing with the plant, even though the stakes feel a lot higher now with health inspectors and everything. But no, it goes way, way back. Like, my mom actually still has this whole box of my old school stuff in her basement—she keeps everything, you know how moms are—and she was just looking through it recently. She pulled out these old report cards from elementary school, I think specifically around fourth grade? And she was reading them to me, and it was honestly a little embarrassing, but also very validating, I guess? \n\nEvery single teacher wrote almost the exact same thing. They’d say I was bright, that I caught on quickly, which was nice to hear, but then there was always a \"but.\" It was always, \"She needs constant reminders to finish her classwork,\" or \"She struggles to turn in homework.\" And I remember that so clearly. I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. But when it came time to actually do the worksheet or write the paragraph, I’d just... drift. I’d miss whole chunks of instructions. The teacher would say, \"Do steps one through five,\" and I’d somehow only hear step one, or I’d do step one and two and then just completely forget there were three more steps. Unless the teacher was physically hovering near my desk, tapping on my paper or specifically checking in on me, the work just didn’t get finished. It’s like, if they walked away, my brain just went, \"Okay, we're done here,\" even if the paper was mostly blank. I mean, I wasn't one of those kids who was always jumping out of their seat or running around the classroom, you know? He was always up at the pencil sharpener, or looking out the window, or just wandering around the back of the room by the cubbies, and the teacher was constantly having to tell him to sit back down. But that wasn't me at all. \n\nI was actually pretty quiet and still, physically speaking. I would sit right there at my desk, exactly where I was supposed to be. The problem wasn't my body getting up and leaving the chair, it was my brain getting up and leaving the room, if that makes sense. Like, I’d be sitting there, hands folded, looking totally compliant, but inside my head, I was a million miles away, thinking about a book I read, or what I was going to do after school, or just staring at the grain on the wooden desk. So, yeah, I never really got in trouble for getting out of my seat or being disruptive like that. I could sit there for the whole hour, no problem... I just wouldn't necessarily be doing what I was supposed to be doing while I was sitting there. But physically staying in the chair? Yeah, I was fine with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have the same trouble finishing things at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Um... you know, I don't think so? Like, my brain is my brain no matter where I am. But my apartment—I live in this little one-bedroom place, it's actually a really old building, lots of exposed brick which I love, though it gets super dusty—anyway, when I'm there, I don't really hit that same wall. \n\nLike, if I decide I'm going to clean the kitchen, I'll actually just clean the kitchen. I'll wash all the dishes, dry them, put them away in the cabinets. I actually organized my plates and bowls by color a few months ago after I finished my bachelor's degree and finally had some free time. I didn't get halfway through the dishes and wander off to reorganize my closet or something. Or like, paying bills. I sit down at my laptop, open up the tabs for the electric company and my internet provider, and I just pay them. I don't get distracted by, you know, an article about how to propagate succulents, which is something I've been trying to do lately, but that's beside the point. \n\nThe point is, when I'm at home, or running errands, or just doing my own thing, I don't have that feeling of... of my brain just slipping off the track, I guess? I can make a grocery list, go to the store, get everything on the list, and come home. It's really just at the plant, with the batch logs and the temperature checks and the sanitation records. It's so frustrating because it's the one place where I really, really need to be on top of things, and it's the one place where I just can't seem to finish what I start. But yeah, outside of work? No, I'd say I'm pretty okay. I don't really struggle with it in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing the paperwork at work, do you find yourself feeling very nervous or worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit there panicking or anything like that. It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating over the clipboard. Which, by the way, the clipboard is always freezing cold anyway because my station is right near the blast freezers. I work with the poultry batches, so obviously everything has to be kept at exactly the right temperature to prevent bacteria, which is exactly why there are so many logs to fill out in the first place. Every thirty minutes, someone has to sign off on the ambient room temp and the internal meat temp, and it's just this endless stack of paper. \n\nBut when I'm actually holding that clipboard, I'm not... I'm not worried about it. I'm not sitting there thinking, \"Oh no, what if I mess this up?\" or \"What if the supervisor yells at me?\" I know exactly *how* to do the paperwork. It's literally just writing down numbers and checking boxes. It's more like... it's just so incredibly tedious that my brain just slides right off the page. \n\nI'll be looking at the little grids where I'm supposed to write the time and the lot number, and instead of feeling anxious, I just feel... I don't know, completely disconnected? Like I'll start looking at the condensation dripping down the metal walls, or I'll start calculating how many chickens probably go through the line in a single twelve-hour shift, or I'll go back to thinking about the color-coded plates in my kitchen cabinets. There's no fear or worry there at all. Just a complete lack of traction. \n\nI know some of the other workers get really stressed about the compliance paperwork because the USDA inspectors are super strict, and if you miss a line or smudge a number, it can be this huge ordeal for the whole shift. But honestly, I don't get that nervous energy about it. I just literally cannot keep my eyes and my mind on the paper long enough to finish the column without zoning out completely. So, yeah, no anxiety or worrying, just... just a lot of staring off into space while holding a really cold pen.\n\nDoctor: In college, did you often have the same trouble finishing assignments or turning in work on time?\n\nPatient: In college? Um, well, college was... I mean, I got my bachelor's degree, obviously, so I got through it. But it wasn't really the same kind of work, you know? Like, writing a paper about agricultural economics is a lot different than standing in a freezing room with a clipboard trying to remember if I checked the ambient temp at 10:15 or 10:30. In college, I could sort of hyper-focus on things if I was interested in them, or I'd just pull an all-nighter right before the deadline. It was stressful, but I got it done.\n\nBut what's happening now at the plant is just... it's why I'm here, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep starting the sanitation steps, the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and somehow I just leave out the last parts. And it's not like I don't know how important it is! I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. The USDA does not mess around, and if they pull our logs and see blank spaces or missing signatures, the whole line can get shut down, which costs the company thousands of dollars a minute. I know all of this. I've sat through the training videos, I've read the manuals, I know exactly what's at stake. \n\nBut I'll be standing there, doing the sanitation check—which involves making sure every single surface on the processing line is clear of debris before the next batch of poultry comes through—and I'll check the main conveyor, the side rails, the catch pans, and then... I just stop. I'll walk away to grab a new pair of gloves, or I'll look at the clock, and I just completely forget to check the underneath of the belt, which is literally the last step. Or with the batch logs, I'll fill out the time, the lot number, the initial temp, the secondary temp, and then I just... don't sign my name at the bottom. I just leave it blank and put the clipboard back. \n\nIt's so incredibly frustrating because I am not a stupid person. I have a degree! I know how to fill out a form. But it's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time. And my supervisor, Dave, he's been getting really annoyed with me. He pulled me aside last week and was like, \"You're doing 90% of the work and failing the assignment,\" which, ironically, sounds like something a college professor would say, but Dave is this big guy with a huge beard who used to work on oil rigs, so it's a lot more intimidating coming from him. But he's right! I keep starting these crucial paperwork tasks and just... not finishing them, even though I know I could get fired for it. It's just bizarre, and I don't know why I keep doing it.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did these attention problems ever cause trouble for you in any setting besides school?\n\nPatient: As a child? Um... outside of school... you know, I really don't think so. I mean, if I really think back to when I was little, like elementary and middle school age, I don't remember having any issues like what I'm dealing with now at the plant. \n\nAt home, for example, my mom was super strict about chores. We had this whole chore wheel on the fridge—she made it out of construction paper and laminated it, with a little metal brad in the middle so it could spin—and if it was my week to vacuum the living room, I just... vacuumed the whole living room. I didn't leave a corner undone or forget to put the vacuum away in the hall closet. I'd actually make sure the carpet had those nice even lines in it, you know? Or like, my extracurriculars. I took gymnastics for a few years, which I was honestly terrible at because I have absolutely zero upper body strength and I was terrified of the balance beam, but I never had trouble paying attention to the coach or finishing the routines. I didn't just wander off the mat halfway through a floor exercise. \n\nIt's so weird to think about, because you'd assume if my brain worked this way, it would have shown up everywhere back then, right? But it really didn't. Whether I was at home doing my chores, or at my grandparents' house in the summer helping my grandpa in his garden, or at gymnastics, I was completely fine. I used to play board games with my younger brother all the time—Monopoly, mostly, which is the worst game because it takes literally hours and hours and always ends in an argument—but I'd sit there the entire time, totally focused, until someone won or one of us flipped the board in frustration. I never just lost the thread of what we were doing. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember any problems with my attention causing trouble for me at home or anywhere else like that when I was growing up. My parents never had to sit me down and tell me I was leaving things half-finished. It really just feels like this bizarre wall I'm hitting specifically with these batch logs and sanitation checks at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Checking it over and over? Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not. Honestly, if I were checking it over and over, Dave probably wouldn't be pulling me aside to tell me I'm missing the signatures at the bottom, right? Because I would actually catch my own mistakes. 

But no, I don't do that at all. It's actually the exact opposite. Once I write a number down, or check a box for the ambient room temp or whatever, that's it. It's completely out of my brain. I don't go back and look at it again. I mean, half the time I'm just trying to get through the column before my brain completely wanders off to think about the condensation on the walls or whatever else catches my eye. 

Plus, it's so incredibly cold in there. Like I said, the blast freezers just make the whole area freezing, so holding that metal clipboard and the pen... my fingers get so stiff inside the gloves. We have to wear these thick gloves, obviously, for safety and hygiene, and trying to flip back through pages of a batch log to double-check my work while wearing them is just so cumbersome. But even taking the gloves and the cold out of the equation, I just really don't have that urge. I don't get that perfectionist, "oh I need to make sure this is exactly right" feeling. 

I just write it—or at least I try to write it—and then I put the clipboard back on its little metal hook by the door. I never stand there reviewing it. Which is probably exactly why I leave so many blanks in the first place, now that I think about it. If I just looked over it one more time, maybe I'd see the missing signature or the blank secondary temp line, but I just... I don't do it. Once I think I'm done, even if I'm only 90% done, I just put it down and walk away. There's no double-checking at all.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. It's nice to meet you too. Um, well, I guess what brings me in today is... it's work stuff, mostly. I work at a meat processing plant, and honestly, the job itself is fine, you know? But there's all this paperwork and protocol, and I just... I keep messing it up. Like, not the actual processing part, but the follow-through. I'll start the sanitation steps—which are super strict, obviously, because of health codes and everything—and I'll get through the first few parts, getting all the equipment sprayed down and whatever, and then somehow, I just... I leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! I'll start filling out the batch logs for the shift, writing down the weights and the times, and then I'll get distracted or I'll just sort of stop before it's actually finished. It's the same with the end-of-line paperwork. I'll have the clipboard right there, I'll start checking off the boxes, and then I'll just walk away or forget to sign off on the bottom section. And it's so frustrating because I *know* my job depends on finishing them correctly. Like, my supervisor has talked to me about it, and I sit there thinking, \"I know I have to do this, I know how important it is,\" but then the next day, I do the exact same thing. It's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time, and I don't know why I can't just push through and finish the whole task.\n\nDoctor: Before you started this job, did you have the same kind of trouble finishing tasks when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, wow, yeah. Actually, yes, absolutely. It’s funny you ask that because it’s not just a new thing with the plant, even though the stakes feel a lot higher now with health inspectors and everything. But no, it goes way, way back. Like, my mom actually still has this whole box of my old school stuff in her basement—she keeps everything, you know how moms are—and she was just looking through it recently. She pulled out these old report cards from elementary school, I think specifically around fourth grade? And she was reading them to me, and it was honestly a little embarrassing, but also very validating, I guess? \n\nEvery single teacher wrote almost the exact same thing. They’d say I was bright, that I caught on quickly, which was nice to hear, but then there was always a \"but.\" It was always, \"She needs constant reminders to finish her classwork,\" or \"She struggles to turn in homework.\" And I remember that so clearly. I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. But when it came time to actually do the worksheet or write the paragraph, I’d just... drift. I’d miss whole chunks of instructions. The teacher would say, \"Do steps one through five,\" and I’d somehow only hear step one, or I’d do step one and two and then just completely forget there were three more steps. Unless the teacher was physically hovering near my desk, tapping on my paper or specifically checking in on me, the work just didn’t get finished. It’s like, if they walked away, my brain just went, \"Okay, we're done here,\" even if the paper was mostly blank. So, yeah, it’s definitely not just the batch logs at work... it’s been happening basically my whole life.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often unable to stay seated when you were expected to remain in your seat?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wasn't one of those kids who was always jumping out of their seat or running around the classroom, you know? Like, I remember there was this one boy in my second-grade class—Tommy, I think his name was—and he literally could not stay in his chair. He was always up at the pencil sharpener, or looking out the window, or just wandering around the back of the room by the cubbies, and the teacher was constantly having to tell him to sit back down. But that wasn't me at all. \n\nI was actually pretty quiet and still, physically speaking. I would sit right there at my desk, exactly where I was supposed to be. The problem wasn't my body getting up and leaving the chair, it was my brain getting up and leaving the room, if that makes sense. Like, I’d be sitting there, hands folded, looking totally compliant, but inside my head, I was a million miles away, thinking about a book I read, or what I was going to do after school, or just staring at the grain on the wooden desk. So, yeah, I never really got in trouble for getting out of my seat or being disruptive like that. I could sit there for the whole hour, no problem... I just wouldn't necessarily be doing what I was supposed to be doing while I was sitting there. But physically staying in the chair? Yeah, I was fine with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have the same trouble finishing things at home?\n\nPatient: At home? Um... you know, I don't think so? Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being an issue like it is at the plant. It's funny because you'd think it would be exactly the same, right? Like, my brain is my brain no matter where I am. But my apartment—I live in this little one-bedroom place, it's actually a really old building, lots of exposed brick which I love, though it gets super dusty—anyway, when I'm there, I don't really hit that same wall. \n\nLike, if I decide I'm going to clean the kitchen, I'll actually just clean the kitchen. I'll wash all the dishes, dry them, put them away in the cabinets. I actually organized my plates and bowls by color a few months ago after I finished my bachelor's degree and finally had some free time. I didn't get halfway through the dishes and wander off to reorganize my closet or something. Or like, paying bills. I sit down at my laptop, open up the tabs for the electric company and my internet provider, and I just pay them. It's so frustrating because it's the one place where I really, really need to be on top of things, and it's the one place where I just can't seem to finish what I start. But yeah, outside of work? No, I'd say I'm pretty okay. I don't really struggle with it in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing the paperwork at work, do you find yourself feeling very nervous or worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit there panicking or anything like that. It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating over the clipboard. Which, by the way, the clipboard is always freezing cold anyway because my station is right near the blast freezers. I work with the poultry batches, so obviously everything has to be kept at exactly the right temperature to prevent bacteria, which is exactly why there are so many logs to fill out in the first place. Every thirty minutes, someone has to sign off on the ambient room temp and the internal meat temp, and it's just this endless stack of paper. \n\nBut when I'm actually holding that clipboard, I'm not... I don't know, completely disconnected? There's no fear or worry there at all. Like, writing a paper about agricultural economics is a lot different than standing in a freezing room with a clipboard trying to remember if I checked the ambient temp at 10:15 or 10:30. In college, I could sort of hyper-focus on things if I was interested in them, or I'd just pull an all-nighter right before the deadline. The USDA does not mess around, and if they pull our logs and see blank spaces or missing signatures, the whole line can get shut down, which costs the company thousands of dollars a minute. I know all of this. I've sat through the training videos, I've read the manuals, I know exactly what's at stake. \n\nBut I'll be standing there, doing the sanitation check—which involves making sure every single surface on the processing line is clear of debris before the next batch of poultry comes through—and I'll check the main conveyor, the side rails, the catch pans, and then... I just stop. I'll walk away to grab a new pair of gloves, or I'll look at the clock, and I just completely forget to check the underneath of the belt, which is literally the last step. Or with the batch logs, I'll fill out the time, the lot number, the initial temp, the secondary temp, and then I just... don't sign my name at the bottom. I just leave it blank and put the clipboard back. \n\nIt's so incredibly frustrating because I am not a stupid person. I have a degree! I know how to fill out a form. But it's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time. And my supervisor, Dave, he's been getting really annoyed with me. He pulled me aside last week and was like, \"You're doing 90% of the work and failing the assignment,\" which, ironically, sounds like something a college professor would say, but Dave is this big guy with a huge beard who used to work on oil rigs, so it's a lot more intimidating coming from him. I'll start the sanitation steps—which are super strict, obviously, because of health codes and everything—and I'll get through the first few parts, getting all the equipment sprayed down and whatever, and then somehow, I just... I leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! I'll start filling out the batch logs for the shift, writing down the weights and the times, and then I'll get distracted or I'll just sort of stop before it's actually finished. And it's so frustrating because I *know* my job depends on finishing them correctly. Like, my supervisor has talked to me about it, and I sit there thinking, \"I know I have to do this, I know how important it is,\" but then the next day, I do the exact same thing. It's like my brain just checks out right before the finish line, every single time, and I don't know why I can't just push through and finish the whole task.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Like, my mom actually still has this whole box of my old school stuff in her basement—she keeps everything, you know how moms are—and she was just looking through it recently. She pulled out these old report cards from elementary school, I think specifically around fourth grade? And she was reading them to me, and it was honestly a little embarrassing, but also very validating, I guess? \n\nEvery single teacher wrote almost the exact same thing. They’d say I was bright, that I caught on quickly, which was nice to hear, but then there was always a \"but.\" It was always, \"She needs constant reminders to finish her classwork,\" or \"She struggles to turn in homework.\" And I remember that so clearly. I would sit in class, and I’d understand the math lesson or the reading assignment perfectly fine. I wasn't confused by the material at all. But when it came time to actually do the worksheet or write the paragraph, I’d just... drift. I’d miss whole chunks of instructions. The teacher would say, \"Do steps one through five,\" and I’d somehow only hear step one, or I’d do step one and two and then just completely forget there were three more steps. Unless the teacher was physically hovering near my desk, tapping on my paper or specifically checking in on me, the work just didn’t get finished. It’s like, if they walked away, my brain just went, \"Okay, we're done here,\" even if the paper was mostly blank. So, yeah, it’s definitely not just the batch logs at work... it’s been happening basically my whole life.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not really. I mean, I wasn't one of those kids who was always jumping out of their seat or running around the classroom, you know? Like, I remember there was this one boy in my second-grade class—Tommy, I think his name was—and he literally could not stay in his chair. He was always up at the pencil sharpener, or looking out the window, or just wandering around the back of the room by the cubbies, and the teacher was constantly having to tell him to sit back down. But that wasn't me at all. \n\nI was actually pretty quiet and still, physically speaking. I would sit right there at my desk, exactly where I was supposed to be. The problem wasn't my body getting up and leaving the chair, it was my brain getting up and leaving the room, if that makes sense. Like, I’d be sitting there, hands folded, looking totally compliant, but inside my head, I was a million miles away, thinking about a book I read, or what I was going to do after school, or just staring at the grain on the wooden desk. So, yeah, I never really got in trouble for getting out of my seat or being disruptive like that. I could sit there for the whole hour, no problem... I just wouldn't necessarily be doing what I was supposed to be doing while I was sitting there. But physically staying in the chair? Yeah, I was fine with that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNervous? Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit there panicking or anything like that. It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating over the clipboard. Which, by the way, the clipboard is always freezing cold anyway because my station is right near the blast freezers. I work with the poultry batches, so obviously everything has to be kept at exactly the right temperature to prevent bacteria, which is exactly why there are so many logs to fill out in the first place. Every thirty minutes, someone has to sign off on the ambient room temp and the internal meat temp, and it's just this endless stack of paper. \n\nBut when I'm actually holding that clipboard, I'm not... I'm not worried about it. I'm not sitting there thinking, \"Oh no, what if I mess this up?\" or \"What if the supervisor yells at me?\" I know exactly *how* to do the paperwork. It's literally just writing down numbers and checking boxes. It's more like... it's just so incredibly tedious that my brain just slides right off the page. \n\nI'll be looking at the little grids where I'm supposed to write the time and the lot number, and instead of feeling anxious, I just feel... I don't know, completely disconnected? Like I'll start looking at the condensation dripping down the metal walls, or I'll start calculating how many chickens probably go through the line in a single twelve-hour shift, or I'll go back to thinking about the color-coded plates in my kitchen cabinets. There's no fear or worry there at all. Just a complete lack of traction. \n\nI know some of the other workers get really stressed about the compliance paperwork because the USDA inspectors are super strict, and if you miss a line or smudge a number, it can be this huge ordeal for the whole shift. But honestly, I don't get that nervous energy about it. I just literally cannot keep my eyes and my mind on the paper long enough to finish the column without zoning out completely. So, yeah, no anxiety or worrying, just... just a lot of staring off into space while holding a really cold pen.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

I just leave it blank and put the clipboard back. \n\nIt's so incredibly frustrating because I am not a stupid person. I have a degree! I know how to fill out a form. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nAs a child? Um... outside of school... you know, I really don't think so. I mean, if I really think back to when I was little, like elementary and middle school age, I don't remember having any issues like what I'm dealing with now at the plant. \n\nAt home, for example, my mom was super strict about chores. We had this whole chore wheel on the fridge—she made it out of construction paper and laminated it, with a little metal brad in the middle so it could spin—and if it was my week to vacuum the living room, I just... vacuumed the whole living room. I didn't leave a corner undone or forget to put the vacuum away in the hall closet. I'd actually make sure the carpet had those nice even lines in it, you know? Or like, my extracurriculars. I took gymnastics for a few years, which I was honestly terrible at because I have absolutely zero upper body strength and I was terrified of the balance beam, but I never had trouble paying attention to the coach or finishing the routines. I didn't just wander off the mat halfway through a floor exercise. \n\nIt's so weird to think about, because you'd assume if my brain worked this way, it would have shown up everywhere back then, right? But it really didn't. Whether I was at home doing my chores, or at my grandparents' house in the summer helping my grandpa in his garden, or at gymnastics, I was completely fine. I used to play board games with my younger brother all the time—Monopoly, mostly, which is the worst game because it takes literally hours and hours and always ends in an argument—but I'd sit there the entire time, totally focused, until someone won or one of us flipped the board in frustration. I never just lost the thread of what we were doing. \n\nSo, no, I really don't remember any problems with my attention causing trouble for me at home or anywhere else like that when I was growing up. My parents never had to sit me down and tell me I was leaving things half-finished. It really just feels like this bizarre wall I'm hitting specifically with these batch logs and sanitation checks at work.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
